    &= - e^{-i \Delta(k,l | i,j) x t} e^{-i \Delta(i,j | k,l) x' t} \abs{\bra{k,l} G \ket{i,j}}^2 - e^{-i \Delta(k,l | i, j) x' t} e^{-i \Delta(i,j | k,l) x t} \abs{\bra{k,l} G \ket{i,j}}^2 \\
    &= - \left(e^{-i \Delta(i, j| k,l) (x' - x) t} + e^{i \Delta(i,j| k,l) (x' - x) t} \right) \abs{\bra{k,l} G \ket{i,j}}^2 \\
    &= - 2 \cos \left( \Delta(i,j | k, l) (x' - x) t \right) \abs{\bra{k,l} G \ket{i,j}}^2,
    \end{align}
    where we used the fact that $\Delta(i,j |k,l) = - \Delta(k,l| i,j)$. Now we can plug this in to Eq. \eqref{eq:second_order_new_1} to get 
    \begin{align}
        \bra{k,l} \mathcal{T}_2(\ketbra{i,j}{i,j} &= \alpha^2 t^2 \abs{\bra{i,j} G \ket{k,l}}^2 \int_0^1 \int_0^1 \cos(\Delta(i,j | k,l) (x' - x) t) dx dx'.
    \end{align}
    The cosine integral can be simplified to a sinc function as follows
    \begin{align}
        \int_0^1 \int_0^1 \cos(\Delta(i,j | k,l) (x' - x) t) dx dx' &= 2\frac{1 - \cos(\Delta(i,j| k,l) t)}{\Delta(i,j| k,l)^2 t^2}  \\
        &= \frac{\sin^2 (\Delta(i,j |k, l) t/2)}{\Delta(i,j | k,l)^2 t^2 /4} \\
        &= \sinc^2(\Delta(i,j | k,l) t/2).
    \end{align}
    This is sufficient to yield the theorem statement.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Remainder Bound}
In order to prove the remainder bound we will need the following lemma. 
\begin{lemma}
    \begin{equation}
         \norm{\partial_\alpha^k G( t)} \le (4 t)^k \norm{G}^{k + 1}.
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma} \label{lem:remainder_bound_interaction_norm}
\begin{proof}
    We prove so inductively, starting with the base case of $k = 1$. For this we just plug in for the Heisenberg derivatives
    \begin{align}
        \norm{\partial_\alpha G( t) } &=  \norm{i  t \int_0^1 [G(t), G(x t)] dx'} \le t \int_0^1  \norm{[G(t), G(x t)]} dx  \le 2 t \norm{G}^2 \int_0^1   dx = 2 t \norm{G}^2.
    \end{align}
    So the base case holds. The inductive step is proved similarly by just plugging in for the derivative at a single layer
    \begin{align}
        \norm{\partial_\alpha^k G( t)} &= \norm{i  t \partial_\alpha^{k - 1}\int_0^1[G(t), G( x t)] dx } \\
        &\le t  \int_0^1 \norm{\partial_\alpha^{k - 1} [G(t), G( x t)]} dx  \\
        &\le t  \int_0^1  \norm{[\partial_\alpha^{k-1} G( t), G( x t)]} + \norm{[G(t) , \partial_{\alpha}^{k-1} G(x t)]} dx \\
        &\le 2 t \norm{G} \norm{\partial_{\alpha}^{k -1} G(t)} + 2 t \norm{G} \int_0^1 \norm{\partial_{\alpha}^{k - 1} G(x t)} d x \\
        &\le 2 t \norm{G} (4 t)^{k - 1} \norm{G}^k + 2t \norm{G} (4 t)^{k -1} \norm{G}^{k} \int_0^1 x^{k - 1} d x \\
        &= 2 \cdot 4^{k - 1} t^{k} \norm{G}^{k + 1} \left(1 + \frac{1}{k} \right) \\
        &\le  (4 t)^k \norm{G}^{k + 1}.
    \end{align}
    Completing the lemma.
\end{proof}
We now wish to use this lemma to prove a bound on the 1 norm of the remainder for a Taylor series of $\boldsymbol{\rho}(t)$. For this we will need to bound the Schätten-1 norm, which we prove below.  We again will do so inductively, making heavy use of Hölder's inequality (specifically $\norm{A B}_1 \le \norm{A} \norm{B}_1$ where the 1 can be put on $A$ or $B$) and the fact that $\norm{\boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}_1 = 1$. This latter fact comes from the unitary invariance of the trace norm and the fact that density matrices have positive eigenvalues that sum to 1, giving a unit trace norm. 

\begin{theorem}
    Remainder error.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The base case $k = 1$ is proven similarly to the proof of Lemma \ref{lem:remainder_bound_interaction_norm}
\begin{align}
    \norm{\partial_\alpha \boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}_1 &=  \norm{i t\int_0^1 [\boldsymbol{\rho(t)}, \boldsymbol{G}(x t)] dx }_1 \le t \int_0^1 \norm{\boldsymbol{\rho}(t) \boldsymbol{G}(xt)}_1 + \norm{\boldsymbol{G}(xt) \boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}_1 dx \le 2 t \norm{G}.
\end{align}
And the inductive step also works out rather similarly
\begin{align}
    &\norm{\partial_\alpha^{k} \boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}_1 \\
    &= \norm{i t \partial_{\alpha}^{k - 1} \int_0^1 \boldsymbol{\rho}(t) \boldsymbol{G}(xt) - \boldsymbol{G}(xt) \boldsymbol{\rho}(t) dx }_1 \\
    &\le t \int_0^1 \norm{(\partial_\alpha^{k - 1} \boldsymbol{\rho}(t) ) \boldsymbol{G}(xt) + \boldsymbol{\rho}(t) (\partial_\alpha^{k - 1} \boldsymbol{G}(xt)) - (\partial_\alpha^{k -1} \boldsymbol{G}(xt)) \boldsymbol{\rho}(t) - \boldsymbol{G}(xt) (\partial_\alpha^{k-  1} \boldsymbol{\rho}(t))}_1 dx \\
    &\le 2 t \norm{G} \norm{\partial_\alpha^{k - 1} \boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}_1 + 2 t \int_0^1 \norm{\partial_{\alpha}^{k - 1} \boldsymbol{G}(xt)} dx \\
    &\le 2 t \norm{G} \norm{\partial_\alpha^{k - 1} \boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}_1 + 2 t (4 t)^{k - 1} \norm{G}^k \int_0^1 x^{k - 1} d x \\
    &\le 2 t \norm{G} \left( \norm{\partial_\alpha^{k - 1} \boldsymbol{\rho}(t)}_1 + (4t \norm{G})^{k - 1} \right) \\
    &\le \left(4 t \norm{G} \right)^k.
\end{align}
The last step is to note that the above bounds hold regardless of the value of $\alpha$, so we can use the Lagrange form of Taylor's remainder to write
\begin{align}
    \norm{R_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^k (\alpha)}_1 = \norm{\partial_\alpha^{k + 1} \boldsymbol{\rho}(t) } \frac{\alpha^{k + 1}}{(k + 1)!} \le \frac{(4 \alpha t)^{k + 1}}{(k + 1)!}  \norm{G}^{k + 1}.
\end{align}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Coherences}
In this section we want to show that if the input state does not contain coherences between energy eigenstates then the second order output also does not have coherences. This is dependent on the properties of the ensemble from which we choose $G$. For first order we need that every matrix element of $G$ is 0 in expectation. For the second order we need that there are no covariances between entries. 

\begin{lemma}
    Provided that each matrix element of $G$ is 0 in expectation, i.e. $\EE_G \bra{i,j} G \ket{k,l} = 0$, then 
    \begin{equation}
        \EE_G \bra{i',j'} \mathcal{T}_1 (\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}) \ket{k',l'} = 0.
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We can actually prove a stronger claim given this assumption, which is that the first order output for all states is 0. This comes from the Heisenberg derivative 
    \begin{align}
        \EE_G \mathcal{T}_1(\rho) = i t \EE_G \int_0^1 [\rho(t), G(xt)] dx = i t \int_0^1 [\rho(t), e^{-i H x t} (\EE_G G ) e^{i H x t}] dx = 0.
    \end{align}
\end{proof}


To show that no coherences are introduced to second order we unsurprisingly need a bit more constraints on the ensemble average of the random matrices. As we will be dealing with two entries of $G$ it turns out to require independence among different entries of a given column (or row since $G^\dagger = G$). This is clearly a weaker condition than total independence of entries, so if one has stronger results of independence among all entries then the following theorem will also apply. We note that this does not guarantee that a state will not have any coherences at all, only that the channel does not introduce any \emph{new} coherences to this order.
\begin{theorem}
    Let $G$ be chosen randomly from an ensemble of Hermitian matrices such that the matrix elements in a given column are independent, expressed formally as
    \begin{equation}
        (i', j') \neq (k', l') \implies \EE_G \bra{i', j'} G \ket{i, j} \overline{\bra{k', l'} G \ket{i,j }} = 0, \label{eq:second_moment_independence}
    \end{equation}
    where the overline represents complex conjugation. This condition implies that the second order dynamics do not introduce any coherences in expectation to a state that is diagonal in the energy eigenbasis. Formally, if $\rho = \sum_{i,j} \rho_{i,j} \ketbra{i,j}{i,j}$ and the condition in Eq. \eqref{eq:second_moment_independence} is met then
    \begin{align}
        (i', j') \neq (k', l') \implies \EE_G \bra{i',j'}  \partial_{\alpha}^2 \boldsymbol{\rho}(t) \big|_{\alpha = 0} \ket{k',l'} = 0.
    \end{align}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    We will show that the expectation is 0 for an input basis state $\ketbra{i,j}{i, j}$ and let linearity carry the argument to any convex combination of basis states. Plugging in $\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}$ for the $\rho$ in Eq. \eqref{eq:second_order_derivative_new_1} gives
\begin{align}
    \EE_G &\partial_\alpha^2 \left(e^{-i (H + \alpha G)t} \ketbra{i,j}{i,j} e^{i (H + \alpha G)t} \right)\bigg|_{\alpha = 0} \nonumber \\
    &= - t^2 \EE_G \int_0^1 \int_0^1 [[\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}, G(x't)], G(xt)] + x [\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}, [G(xt), G(x'xt)]] dx dx'. \label{eq:coherences_tmp_0}
\end{align}
We will first analyze the leftmost term. Computing the overlap with $\ketbra{i', j'}{k',l'}$ for $(i',j') \neq (k',l')$ and reducing the expression to matrix elements of $G$ gives
\begin{align}
    &\bra{i',j'} [[\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}, G(x't)], G(xt)] \ket{k',l'} \\
    &= \braket{i', j'}{i,j} \bra{i,j}G(x't) G(xt) \ket{k',l'} - \bra{i', j'} G(xt) \ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} G(x't) \ket{k',l'} \\
    &- \bra{i',j'} G(x't) \ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} G(xt) \ket{k',l'} + \bra{i', j'} G(xt) G(x't) \ket{i,j} \braket{i,j}{k', l'} \\
    &= \sum_{m, n} \braket{i',j'}{i,j} e^{-i (\Delta(i,j| m,n) x' + \Delta(m,n | k',l')x) t} \bra{i,j}G\ket{m,n} \bra{m,n} G\ket{k',l'} \nonumber \\
    &+\sum_{m,n} \braket{i,j}{k',l'} e^{-i (\Delta(i',j'| m,n)x + \Delta(m,n|i,j) x')t } \bra{i',j'}G \ket{m,n} \bra{m,n} G \ket{i,j} \nonumber \\
    &- e^{-i (\Delta(i',j'|i,j) x + \Delta(i,j|k',l') x' )t } \bra{i',j'} G \ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} G \ket{k', l'} \nonumber \\
    &- e^{-i(\Delta(i', j'| i,j) x' + \Delta(i,j | k', l') x)t} \bra{i',j'}G\ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} G \ket{k', l'}. \label{eq:coherences_tmp_1}
\end{align}
The expectation value of each of these terms is 0 from our assumption, writing the first term explicitly
\begin{align}
    &\EE_G \sum_{m,n} \braket{i',j'}{i,j} e^{-i (\Delta(i,j| m,n) x' + \Delta(m,n | k',l')x) t} \bra{i,j}G\ket{m,n} \bra{m,n} G\ket{k',l'} \\
    &=\sum_{m,n} e^{-i (\Delta(i,j| m,n) x' + \Delta(m,n | k',l')x) t}  \EE_G \bra{i', j'} G \ketbra{m,n}{m,n} G \ket{k', l'} = 0,
\end{align}
where the independence constraint holds for all $(m,n)$, yielding 0 for the entire sum. The second term can be seen to be 0 through the same argument and the third and fourth terms in Eq. \eqref{eq:coherences_tmp_1} are even more explicit. 

The final argument we need is that the Jacobi identity can be used to convert the second term in Eq. \eqref{eq:coherences_tmp_0} into the exact same form as the first term
\begin{align}
    &\EE_G \bra{i',j'} [\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}, [G(xt), G(x' x t)]] \ket{k',l'} \nonumber \\
    &= \EE_G \bra{i',j'}[[\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}, G(xt)], G(x' x t)] \ket{k',l'} - \EE_G \bra{i',j'} [[\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}, G(x' x t)], G(xt)] \ket{k',l'}.
\end{align}
By the above argument yielding 0 for Eq. \eqref{eq:coherences_tmp_1} we see that each of these two terms are also 0, and thus the theorem statement holds. 
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    Let $G \sim U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger \Lambda U_{\rm Cliff}$, where $\Lambda$ is distributed according to
    \begin{align}
        \Lambda \sim (-1)^{b_0} Z_1^{b_1} \tp \ldots \tp Z_{n}^{b_n} \tp Z_E^{b_e} \quad \text{with} \quad \Pr[b_i = 0] = \Pr[b_i = 1] = \frac{1}{2}
    \end{align}
    and $U_{\rm Cliff}$ is a random Clifford circuit. Then the following two conditions hold
    \begin{align}
        \EE_G \bra{i,j} G \ket{k, l} &= 0 \\
        \EE_G \bra{i',j' } G \ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} G \ket{k', l'} &=  \frac{\delta_{(i', j'), (k', l')}}{\dim + 1}.
    \end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The first order expectation follows from the fact that we have a random sign for $\Lambda$
\begin{align}
    \EE_G \bra{i,j} G \ket{k,l} &= \EE_{b_0} (-1)^{b_0} \EE_{b_i} \EE_{\rm Cliff} \bra{i,j} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger \tp Z_i^{b_i} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{k,l} \nonumber \\
    &= \frac{1}{2}  \EE_{b_i} \EE_{\rm Cliff} \bra{i,j} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger \tp Z_i^{b_i} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{k,l} - \frac{1}{2}  \EE_{b_i} \EE_{\rm Cliff} \bra{i,j} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger \tp Z_i^{b_i} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{k,l} \nonumber \\
    &= 0.
\end{align}
The second order expectation follows from the fact that the Clifford group forms a 3-design \cite{webb2015clifford} and we can therefore replace the Clifford group average with a Haar average. We first break down the expectation into the eigenvalue expectation, as this will reduce the indices needed for the Haar average

To simplify we will compute the eigenvalue covariance first. 

There are some technicalities to deal with. The Pauli matrices are diagonal in the computational basis, which is not the same as the energy eigenbasis. But this does not matter for the Haar averaging as it is invariant under the rotation. First need to show computational basis. Let $\ket{a} = \ket{a_1} \tp \ldots \ket{a_n} \tp \ket{a_E}$ be a computational basis state. Then the average eigenvalue satisfies
\begin{align}
     \Lambda \ket{a}  &= (-1)^{b_0} Z_1^{b_1} \ket{a_1} \tp \ldots \tp Z_n^{b_n} \ket{a_n} \tp Z_E^{b_E} \ket{a_E} \\
    &= (-1)^{b_0} \tp_i (-1)^{a_i b_i} \ket{a_i} \\
    &= (-1)^{b_0} (-1)^{\sum_i a_i b_i} \ket{a}.
\end{align}
This shows that $\lambda_a = (-1)^{b_0 + \sum_{i} a_i b_i}$. We need to compute the covariance
\begin{align}
    \EE_\Lambda \lambda_a \lambda_{a'} &= \EE_{b_i} (-1)^{b_0 + \sum_{i} a_i b_i} (-1)^{b_0 + \sum_{i} a_i' b_i} = \prod_{i = 1}^{E} \EE_{b_i} (-1)^{(a_i + a_i') b_i} = \prod_{i = 1}^E  \delta_{a_i, a_i'} = \delta_{a, a'},
\end{align}
which follows from the fact that $a_i$ can only be 0 or 1. 

We can plug in to our overlap, reminding the reader that the $\ket{a}$ are still in the computational basis 
\begin{align}
    &\EE_G \bra{i', j'} G \ket{i,j}\bra{i,j} G \ket{k', l'} \\
    &= \EE_{\Lambda} \EE_{\rm Cliff} \sum_{a, a'} \lambda_{a} \lambda_{a'} \bra{i',j'} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger \ket{a} \bra{a} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger \ket{a'} \bra{a'} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{k',l'} \\
    &= \sum_a \EE_{\rm Cliff}  \bra{i',j'} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger \ket{a} \bra{a} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger \ket{a} \bra{a} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{k',l'}.
\end{align}
Now let $U_{a, 0}$ be the unitary Householder rotation such that $U_{a,0} \ket{a} = \ket{0, 0}$. Then the above can be rewritten
\begin{align}
    &\EE_G \bra{i', j'} G \ket{i,j}\bra{i,j} G \ket{k', l'} \\
    &= \sum_a \EE_{\rm Cliff}  \bra{i',j'} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger U_{a,0}^\dagger \ket{0,0} \bra{0,0} U_{a,0} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} U_{\rm Cliff}^\dagger U_{a, 0}^\dagger \ket{0,0} \bra{0,0} U_{a,0} U_{\rm Cliff} \ket{k',l'} \\
    &= \sum_{a} \EE_{\rm Haar} \bra{i',j'} U_{\rm Haar}^\dagger \ket{0, 0} \bra{0,0} U_{\rm Haar} \ket{i,j} \bra{i,j} U_{\rm Haar}^\dagger \ket{0,0} \bra{0,0} U_{\rm Haar} \ket{k', l'} \\
    &= \frac{\dim}{\dim^2 - 1} \parens{\delta_{(0,0), (0,0)} \delta_{(i, j), (i',j')} \delta_{(0,0), (0,0)} \delta_{(k',l'), (i,j)} + \delta_{(0,0), (0,0)} \delta_{(i,j), (i,j)} \delta_{(0,0), (0,0)} \delta_{(k', l'), (i', j')}} \nonumber \\
    &\quad - \frac{1}{\dim^2 - 1} \parens{\delta_{(0, 0), (0, 0)} \delta_{(i, j), (i', j')} \delta_{(0,0), (0,0)} \delta_{(k', l'), (i,j)} + \delta_{(0,0), (0,0)} \delta_{(i,j),(i,j)} \delta_{(0,0), (0,0)} \delta_{(k', l'), (i',j')}} \\
    &= \frac{\delta_{(i',j'), (k', l')}}{\dim^2 - 1} (\delta_{(i,j), (i',j')} + 1) \left( \dim - 1  \right) \\
    &= \frac{1}{\dim + 1} \delta_{(i', j'), (k', l')} (1 + \delta_{(i,j), (i', j')}),
\end{align}
where we used the Haar integral result from Brandaõ et al., restated here in Lemma \ref{lem:haar_two_moment} for convenience. We also remind the reader that for $(i,j) = (i',j')$ we have already computed this transition in terms of the non-stationary ones in Lemma \ref{lem:second_order_rates_final}. This completes the proof.

\end{proof}


\section{Markov Chain Reduction}
The thermalizing channel we have constructed can therefore be analyzed by it's action on the Hamiltonian eigenbasis to second order in the coupling constant $\alpha$. We find that due to the action of $\sinc$ there are always some non-zero interactions that we do not want to consider in our analysis. We will call these ``off-resonance" transitions as they are sufficiently far away from the bulk of the dynamics (which we term the ``on-resonance" transitions). First the total output for a given value of $\gamma$
\begin{align}
    \bra{k,l} \mathcal{T}^{(2)} (\ketbra{i,j}{i,j}) \ket{k,l} = \begin{cases}
    \alpha^2 t^2 \sinc^2(\Delta(i, j|k, l) t/ 2) \abs{\bra{i, j} G \ket{k, l}}^2 & \text{ if } (i,j) \neq (k,l) \\
        1 - \sum_{(i', j') \neq (i,j)} \alpha^2 t^2 \sinc^2(\Delta(i, j|i', j') t/ 2) \abs{\bra{i, j} G \ket{i',j'}}^2 & \text{ if } (i,j) = (k,l)
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
Next we introduce the $G$ averaging, the thermal initial state, and the partial trace at the end. For simplicity we only compute the $i \neq j$ system transitions as the stationary transitions follow from trace preservation
\begin{align}
    &\Tr_E \EE_G \bra{j} \Phi^{(2)} (\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j} \\
    &= \alpha^2 t^2 \sum_{e' = 0, 1} \bigg( \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sinc^2\left((\Delta_S(i,j) + \Delta_E(0, e'))t / 2 \right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, e'}}^2 \\
    &+ \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sinc^2((\Delta_S (i,j) + \Delta_E (1, e'))t / 2) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, e'}}^2 \bigg) \\
    &= \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}}  \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j,0}}^2 \sinc^2(\Delta_S (i,j) t /2) \\
    &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j,1}}^2 \sinc^2((\Delta_S (i,j) - \gamma) t /2)  \\
    &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}}  \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j,0}}^2 \sinc^2((\Delta_S (i,j) + \gamma) t /2) \\
    &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}}  \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j,1}}^2 \sinc^2(\Delta_S (i,j) t /2). \label{eq:master_transition}
\end{align}
We now split these four terms into on and off-resonance terms based on $\Delta_S (i,j)$ and the argument of the sinc function. The idea is that as $t$ gets large then the sinc will filter out transitions that do not conserve energy. 

\begin{definition}[Definition of Transition Matrix Elements]\label{def:transition}
The transition elements in Eq. \eqref{eq:transition_terms_total} can be divided into on-resonance and off-resonance terms based on the arguments to the sinc function. We define for $i \neq j$ the on-resonance transitions as
\begin{align}
    \bra{j} \TT_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j} &\coloneqq \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[|\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma| \le \delta_{\min}]  \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma)t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 \nonumber \\
    &~+ \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[|\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma| \le \delta_{\min}]  \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma)t}{2}\right)  \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 \label{eq:on_resonance}
\end{align}
and the off-resonance terms as
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
    \bra{j} \TT_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j} &\coloneqq \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[|\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma| > \delta_{\min}]  \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma)t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 \\
    &~+ \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[|\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma| > \delta_{\min}]  \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma)t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, 0}}^2  \\
    &~+ \alpha^2 t^2 \sinc^2 \left( \frac{\Delta_S(i,j)t}{2} \right) \left(\frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 + \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 \right). \label{eq:off_resonance}
    \end{split}
\end{equation}
For same-state transitions $\ketbra{i}{i}$ to $\ketbra{i}{i}$ the on- and off-resonance transitions are equal to
\begin{equation}
    \bra{i} \TT_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{i} = - \sum_{j \neq i}\bra{j} \TT_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} \text{ and } \bra{i} \TT_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{i} = - \sum_{j \neq i}\bra{j} \TT_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j}.\label{eq:same_state_transition_resonances}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}



To see why we used the term off-resonance we provide the following proof that the magnitude of theses transitions is controllably small. We will show two results, one for a fixed value of $\gamma$ and the other for an averaged variant of $\gamma$ associated with the zero knowledge arguments. 


\begin{lemma}
    The off-resonance terms are upper bounded by the square of the ratio of the interaction strength to the cutoff $\delta_{\min}$, formally
    \begin{align}
        [\rho, H] = 0 \implies \norm{\mathcal{T}_{\rm off}(\rho)}_1 \le 8 \frac{\alpha^2  }{\delta_{\min}^2} \EE_G \norm{G}^2.
    \end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We start by noting that for a diagonal input state the output of the off-resonance terms will also be diagonal. This lets us simplify the trace norm to a sum over transitions as
    \begin{align}
        \norm{\mathcal{T}_{\off}(\rho)}_1 &= \norm{\sum_{j} \ketbra{j}{j} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\rm off} (\rho) \ket{j}} \nonumber \\
        &=\sum_{j} \abs{\bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\off}(\rho) \ket{j}} \\
        &\le \sum_{i, j} \rho_{i,i} \abs{\bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j}} \\
        &= \sum_{i} \rho_{i,i} \left(\sum_{j \neq i} |\bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j}| + |\bra{i} \mathcal{T}_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{i}| \right) \\
        &= \sum_{i} \rho_{i,i} \left(\sum_{j \neq i} |\bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j}| + |- \sum_{j \neq i} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j}| \right) \\
        &\le 2 \sum_{i} \rho_{i,i} \left(\sum_{j \neq i} |\bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\off}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j}| \right). \label{eq:off_resonance_bound_1}
    \end{align}

    Now for the off-resonance terms we can make the following bound on the sinc function using the indicator variable 
    \begin{align}
        \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S (i,j) - \gamma } \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S (i,j) - \gamma) t}{2}\right) &\le \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S (i,j) - \gamma } \ge \delta_{\min}] \frac{4}{t^2 (\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma)^2} \\
        &\le \frac{4}{t^2 \delta_{\min}^2}.
    \end{align}
    Similarly we have $\mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2 \left(\frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma) t}{2} \right) \le \frac{4}{t^2 \delta_{\min}^2}$ and $i \neq j \implies \sinc^2\left( \frac{\Delta_S(i,j) t}{2} \right) \le \frac{4}{t^2 \delta_{\min}^2}$. We can therefore plug in and simplify the off-resonance terms from Eq. \ref{eq:off_resonance} as
    \begin{align}
        \abs{\bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\rm off}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j}} &\le \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sinc^2 \left( \frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma) t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 \mathbf{I}[|\Delta_S (i,j) - \gamma | \ge \delta_{\min}] \nonumber \\ 
        &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sinc^2 \left( \frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma) t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 \mathbf{I}[|\Delta_S (i,j) + \gamma | \ge \delta_{\min}] \nonumber \\ 
        &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \sinc^2 \left( \frac{\Delta_S (i,j) t}{2} \right) \left(\frac{1}{1+ e^{-\beta \gamma}} \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j,0}}^2 + \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j,1}}^2 \right) \nonumber \\
        &\le \frac{4 \alpha^2}{\delta_{\min}^2} \sum_{e, e'} \Pr[e] \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, e} G \ket{j, e'}}^2,
    \end{align}
    where $\Pr[e]$ is just the Boltzmann distribution over the input environment state. We can then plug this back in to Eq. \eqref{eq:off_resonance_bound_1} and get
    \begin{align}
        \norm{\mathcal{T}_{\rm off}(\rho)}_1 &\le \frac{8 \alpha^2}{\delta_{\min}^2} \sum_{i} \rho_{i, i} \sum_{j \neq i} \sum_{e, e'} \Pr[e] \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,e} G \ket{j, e'}}^2 \\
        &\le \frac{8 \alpha^2}{\delta_{\min}^2} \EE_G \sum_{i, e} \rho_{i, i} \Pr[e] \EE_G \sum_{j, e'} \bra{i, e} G^\dagger \ketbra{j, e'}{j,e'} G \ket{i,e} \\
        &= \frac{8 \alpha^2}{\delta_{\min}^2} \EE_G \sum_{i, e} \rho_{i, i} \Pr[e] \EE_G \bra{i, e} G^\dagger G \ket{i,e} \\
        &\le \frac{8 \alpha^2 \EE_G\norm{G}^2}{\delta_{\min}^2}.
    \end{align}
\end{proof}

Next we show that if we focus only on the diagonal of the density matrix of the system then it undergoes Markovian dynamics over each interaction. 

\begin{lemma}[Quantum Dynamics to Classical Markov Chain] \label{lem:quantum_to_classical}
    Let $T$ be the matrix defined by 
    \begin{equation}
        e_i^T T e_j \coloneqq \bra{i} \mathcal{T}_{\on}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{i}.
    \end{equation}
    The matrix $I + T$ models the transitions of the quantum channel over repeated applications 
    \begin{equation}
        \bra{j} (\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on})^{\circ L} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} = e_j^T (I + T)^L e_i
    \end{equation}
    and by linearity of $\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on}$ extends to any diagonal density matrix input $\rho = \sum_i p(i) \ketbra{i}{i}$. The matrix $I + T$ is a column stochastic Markov transition matrix provided that $\alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \norm{G}^2 \le 1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We prove this inductively on $L$. The base case of $L = 1$ is trivial from the defintion of $T$
    \begin{align}
    \bra{j} (\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on})(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} = \delta_{i,j} + \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} = e_j^T (I +  T) e_i.
\end{align}
For the inductive step we will rely on the fact that there are no off-diagonal elements for diagonal inputs. 
\begin{align}
    \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{k} = \delta_{j,k} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} \implies \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{\circ L} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{k} = \delta_{j,k} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{\circ L} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j}.
\end{align}
This is again by induction where the case $L = 1$ is proved in Theorem \ref{thm:second_order_transition} and the inductive step is 
\begin{align}
    \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{\circ L} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{k} &= \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on} \left( \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{\circ L - 1} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \right) \ket{k} \\
    &= \sum_{m, n} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}\left( \ketbra{m}{m}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{\circ L - 1}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ketbra{n}{n}\right) \ket{k} \\
    &= \sum_{m, n} \delta_{m,n} \bra{m}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{\circ L - 1}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{m} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}\left(   \ketbra{m}{m} \right) \ket{k} \\
    &= \sum_{m} \bra{m}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{\circ L - 1}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{m} \delta_{j,k} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}\left(   \ketbra{m}{m} \right) \ket{j} \\
    &= \delta_{j,k} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{\circ L} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j}.
\end{align} 
This argument points the way towards how we will prove the inductive step in our stochastic conversion, starting with
\begin{align}
    \bra{j} (\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on})^{\circ L}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} &= \bra{j} \left( (\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on})^{\circ L - 1} (\ketbra{i}{i}) + \mathcal{T}_{\on} \circ (\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on})^{\circ L - 1} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \right)\ket{j} \\
    &= e_j^T (\identity + T)^{L - 1} e_i + \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on} \circ (\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on})^{\circ L - 1} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} . \label{eq:matrix_reloaded1}
\end{align}
We can use the inductive hypothesis on the term on the left and we now have to break down the $\mathcal{T}_{\on}$ term. 
\begin{align}
    \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on} \circ (\identity + \mathcal{T})^{\circ L - 1} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} &= \sum_{m, n} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}\left( \ketbra{m}{m} (\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on})^{\circ L - 1} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ketbra{n}{n} \right) \ket{j} \\
    &= \sum_{m} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on} \left( \ketbra{m}{m} \right) \ket{j} e_m^T (I + T)^{L - 1} e_i \\
    &= \sum_m e_j^T T e_m e_m^T (I + T)^{L -1} e_i \\
    &= e_j^T T(I + T)^{L-1} e_i.
\end{align}
Substituting this into Eq. \eqref{eq:matrix_reloaded1} yields \begin{equation}
     \bra{j} (\identity + \mathcal{T}_{\on})^{\circ L}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} = e_j^T (I + T)^{L} e_i.
\end{equation} 

Next we show that each entry in $I + T$ is positive and less than 1. For $i \neq j$ we have
\begin{align}
    e_j^\top (I + T) e_i &= e_j^\top T e_i \nonumber \\
    &= \bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j} \nonumber \\
    & =\alpha^2 t^2 \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2 \left( \frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma) t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j,1}}^2 \nonumber \\
    &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2 \left( \frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma) t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j,0}}^2.
\end{align}
This is clearly positive and we can obtain a mild condition on $\alpha$, $t$, and $\EE_G \norm{G}$ to ensure the rate is less than 1
\begin{align}
    & \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2 \left( \frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma) t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j,1}}^2 \nonumber \\
    &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2 \left( \frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma) t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j,0}}^2 \nonumber \\
    &\le \alpha^2 t^2 \left( \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j,1}}^2 + \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j,0}}^2\right) \\
    &\le \alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \norm{G}^2.
\end{align}
The key bounds that we used are the fact that both the indicator function and the sinc functions are less than 1. So provided $\alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \norm{G}^2 \le 1$ the transition rate is less than 1.

The trickier part is to show that the stationary rates ($i = j$) are positive. We can see that they are less than one rather straightforwardly
\begin{align}
    e_i^\top (I + T) e_i &= 1 + e_i^\top T e_i = 1 + \bra{i} \mathcal{T}_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{i} = 1 - \sum_{j \neq i} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j}.
\end{align}
Since each of the transition rates in the last term are positive then we know that the stationary rate is less than 1. To show it is positive we need 
\begin{align}
    0 \le 1 - \sum_{j \neq i} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j} \iff
    \sum_{j \neq i} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} \le 1.
\end{align}
At first glance this may seem difficult to show without picking up a factor of the dimension, as the only other bound we have is that each rate is less than $\alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \norm{G}^2$. However, it turns out that the upper bound of each transition rate by the spectral norm is rather loose and in fact the sum over all $j$ can be absorbed by the transition rate squared. This leads us to a similar upper bound to before as
\begin{align}
    &\sum_{j \neq i} \bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\on}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j} \nonumber \\
    &= \sum_{j \neq i}\alpha^2 t^2 \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2 \left( \frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma) t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j,1}}^2 \nonumber \\
    &+ \sum_{j \neq i}\alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2 \left( \frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma) t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j,0}}^2 \\
    &\le \alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \left(\frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sum_{j \neq i} \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 + \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sum_{j \neq i} \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 \right) \\
    &\le \alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \left(\frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sum_{j, e'} \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, e'}}^2 + \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sum_{j, e'} \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, e'}}^2 \right) \\
    &= \alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \left(\frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sum_{j, e'} \bra{i, 0} G^\dagger \ket{j, e'} \bra{j,e'} G \ket{i, 0} + \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sum_{j, e'} \bra{i, 1} G^\dagger \ket{j, e'} \bra{j, e'} G \ket{i, 1} \right) \\
    &\le \alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \norm{G}^2,
\end{align}
where we note that the upper bound resulting from the inclusion of the $j = i$ term and the sum over the environment states $e'$ is possible as all prefactors are positive. We therefore have that the same condition that leads to transition rates being less than 1 also leads to the stationary rates being positive.

Our final step in the proof is to show that $I + T$ is column-stochastic. This is straightforward from our definition of $T$
\begin{equation}
    \sum_i e_i^T (I + T) e_j = 1 + \sum_i \bra{i} \mathcal{T}_{\on}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{i}.
\end{equation}
Now we use the fact that $\bra{j} \TT_{\on}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{j} = - \sum_{i \neq j} \bra{i} \TT_{\on}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{i}$ from Eq. \eqref{eq:same_state_transition_resonances} to conclude that $I + T$ is column stochastic.
\end{proof}



\subsection{Fixed Points}

To study the convergence of repeated applications of the channel $\Phi$ we will show that the thermal state of the system is an approximate fixed point of the underlying Markov chain. We will focus on the on-resonance transition matrix, as we know that we can suppress the off-resonance and higher order $\alpha$ terms by taking a sufficiently small $\alpha$. We will be able to show that if one has perfect knowledge of the energy gaps $\Delta_S(i,j)$ then the thermal state is \emph{exactly} fixed for the on-resonance dynamics, and further that the ground state is always a fixed point, regardless of environment gap tuning. 

Assuming an input state $\rho$ that commutes with $H$, we want to know if the thermal state $\rho_\beta$ is fixed, which would imply that
\begin{equation}
    \norm{\rho(\beta) - \Phi(\rho(\beta))}_1 = 0.
\end{equation}
In a previous version of this paper we isolated various ``on-resonance" and ``off-resonance" transitions. We were able to show that the off-resonance transitions could be made arbitrarily small relative to the on-resonance peaks and further we were able to identify exact fixed points of the on-resonance dynamics. These fixed points depended on the distribution used for the environment gap $\gamma$, specifically we found that the ground state (or more precisely the lowest energy subspace) is exactly fixed for all distributions and that an arbitrary temperature thermal state is exactly fixed if one samples $\gamma$ uniformly from the Bohr spectrum. 

Although these results are correct and still hold, they involve a complicated splitting of transitions between on and off-resonance and further the thermal state being exactly fixed depends on sampling from the \emph{exact} Bohr frequencies. This is unlikely to be computationally feasible for any physically realistic system. Further, due to the complications of this division into on-resonance and off-resonance transitions the bounds for approximate fixed points were rather poor, they scaled like $O\left(\frac{\alpha^2 t}{\norm{H_S}} e^{\beta \delta_{\min}} \right)$, where $\delta_{\min}$ is the lower bound used to determine an on-resonance threshold and is the smallest difference between Bohr frequencies. Since $\delta_{\min}$ is independent of $\beta$ this implies that our bounds diverge as we send $\beta \to \infty$, a sharp contrast to our earlier results that the ground state is \emph{exactly} fixed when using this on and off resonance treatment.

Instead of using this notion of resonance we will abandon an attempt to find exact fixed points with the goal of improving the bounds for the approximate fixed states. This allows us to develop a precise Fourier based theory of the energy transitions that leads us to significant improvements for the convergence of the approximate fixed points. Asymptotically, our reduction moves from $O\left(\frac{\alpha^2 t}{\norm{H_S}} e^{\beta \delta_{\min}} \right)$ to $O(\frac{\alpha^2 \beta}{\norm{H_S}} \EE_G \norm{G}^2 (\ln (t) + \sum_{i \neq j} \ln (2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta_S(i,j)) + \ln (\Delta_S(i,j))) )$, which is an exponential improvement in $t$ and $\beta$. Previously, a fixed ensemble for $G$ was used which evaluated to $\EE_G \norm{G}^2 = 1$ but our current results are more flexible with respect to the interaction ensemble.  

\subsection{Detailed Balance}
One of the key ideas I've realized is that the ``filter" design actually becomes a lot more transparent whenever we ignore the on-vs-off resonance and just include an integral over the entirety of $\gamma$. This brings up a natural convolution that is a lot easier to deal with than having to bound error terms from this on-vs-off. I want to see if this is worth exploring. For now, make the assumption that $i > j$ which allows us to simplify the transitions in the detailed balance condition, using Eq. \eqref{eq:master_transition} as
\begin{align}
    &\rho_{\beta}(i) \EE_\gamma \bra{j} \Phi^{(2)}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} - \rho_{\beta}(j)\EE_\gamma \bra{i} \Phi^{(2)}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{i} \nonumber \\
    &= \frac{e^{- \beta \lambda_S(i)}}{\partfun_S} \EE_\gamma  \left( \bra{j}\mathcal{T}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j} - e^{\beta \Delta} \bra{i} \mathcal{T}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{i} \right) \\
    &= \alpha^2 t^2 \rho_{\beta}(i) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 \sinc^2(\Delta t/2) \EE_\gamma  \frac{1 - e^{\beta \Delta}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \\
    &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \rho_{\beta}(i)\EE_G  \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 \sinc^2(\Delta t/2) \EE_\gamma  \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}(1 - e^{\beta \Delta})}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \\
    &+ \alpha^2 t^2 \rho_{\beta}(i) \EE_G  \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 \EE_\gamma \sinc^2((\Delta + \gamma) t /2) \left(\frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{ 1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} - \frac{e^{\beta \Delta}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right) \\
    &+\alpha^2 t^2 \rho_{\beta}(i) \EE_G  \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 \EE_\gamma \sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t /2) \left(\frac{1}{ 1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} - \frac{e^{\beta \Delta} e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right).
\end{align}
Our goal is to identify some sufficient conditions such that this quantity converges to 0 and we achieve exact detailed balance. First one major simplification is if we let $G = G_Z \tp Z + G_X \tp X$, where $G_Z^\dagger = G_Z$ and $G_X^\dagger = G_X$. Then in this scenario we have
\begin{align}
    \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 = \abs{\bra{i}G_Z\ket{j}}^2 = \abs{\bra{i, 1}G\ket{j,1}}^2.
\end{align}
Then we can simplify the first two terms in the above to
\begin{align}
    \alpha^2 t^2 \EE_G \abs{\bra{i} G_Z \ket{j}}^2 \sinc^2 (\Delta t /2) (1 - e^{\beta \Delta}),
\end{align}
as we have assumed that there is no $\gamma$ dependence in the interaction operators, which means the gamma average just yields 1. It will prove useful to also rewrite the sinc function as the ratio of the sine over it's argument (which partially cancels the $t^2$ factor), yielding
\begin{align}
    &\rho_{\beta}(i) \EE_\gamma \bra{j} \Phi^{(2)}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} - \rho_{\beta}(j)\EE_\gamma \bra{i} \Phi^{(2)}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{i} \nonumber \\
    &= 4\alpha^2 \rho_{\beta}(i) \EE_G \abs{\bra{i} G_Z \ket{j}}^2 \frac{\sin^2(\Delta t /2)}{\Delta^2} (1 - e^{\beta \Delta}) \\
    &+ 4 \alpha^2 \rho_{\beta}(i) \EE_G  \abs{\bra{i, 1} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 \EE_\gamma \frac{\sin^2((\Delta + \gamma) t /2)}{(\Delta + \gamma)^2} \left(\frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{ 1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} - \frac{e^{\beta \Delta}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right) \\
    &+ 4\alpha^2 \rho_{\beta}(i) \EE_G  \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 \EE_\gamma \frac{\sin^2((\Delta - \gamma) t /2)}{(\Delta - \gamma)^2} \left(\frac{1}{ 1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} - \frac{e^{\beta \Delta} e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right).
\end{align}

We now see one important phenomenon. For nonzero $\alpha$ and $G_Z$ the first term is nonzero and independent of $t$ for all $\beta > 0$ and $\Delta >0$. This implies that no design of repeated interactions can have exact detailed balance if the interactions chosen have some terms that do not flip the qubit. In other words, if the interaction matrix is not block off-diagonal in the environment basis then you cannot have exact detailed balance. 

Now let us investigate the the environment transitioning terms. Each of these will boil down to similar integrals, so for now let us focus on the last term. This can be expressed
\begin{align}
    &\EE_\gamma \frac{\sin^2((\Delta - \gamma) t /2)}{(\Delta - \gamma)^2} \left(\frac{1}{ 1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} - \frac{e^{\beta \Delta} e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right) \\
    &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \Pr[\gamma] \frac{\sin^2((\Delta - \gamma) t /2)}{(\Delta - \gamma)^2} \left(\frac{1}{ 1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} - \frac{e^{\beta \Delta} e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right) d\gamma \\
    &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{\Pr[\gamma]}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma} } \cdot \sin^2 ((\Delta - \gamma) t/2) \frac{1 - e^{\beta (\Delta - \gamma)}}{(\Delta - \gamma)^2} d\gamma.
\end{align}
And here we see that if we define $f(x) = \frac{\Pr[x]}{1 + e^{-\beta x} }$ and $g(x) = \sin^2 (x t/2) \frac{1 - e^{\beta x}}{x^2}$ then we are looking at the convolution of the probabilty that we choose gamma and find it in the ground state with the sin function. 

\subsubsection{Time Dependence}
To proceed we need to study the Fourier spectrum of our two functions. First we look at the spectrum for $g(x)$ as this will be the same regardless of our probability distribution. We have 
\begin{align}
    \hat{g}(\omega) &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} g(x) e^{-i \omega x} d x \\
    &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \sin^2 (x t/2) \frac{1 - e^{\beta x}}{x^2} dx \\
    &= \frac{1}{2 \sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \frac{1 - e^{\beta x}}{x^2} dx - \frac{1}{2 \sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \cos( x t)\frac{1 - e^{\beta x}}{x^2} dx \\
    &=  \frac{1}{2 \sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \frac{1}{x^2} dx - \frac{1}{2 \sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{ -i \omega x} \frac{e^{\beta x}}{x^2} dx \\
    &- \frac{1}{4 \sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \frac{e^{-i t x}}{x^2} dx -\frac{1}{4 \sqrt{2\pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \frac{e^{i t x}}{x^2} dx \\
    &+ \frac{1}{4 \sqrt{2 \pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \frac{e^{(\beta - i  t)x}}{x^2} dx + \frac{1}{4 \sqrt{2 \pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}  e^{-i \omega x} \frac{e^{(\beta + i t)x}}{x^2} dx.
\end{align}
This boils down to the following complex valued integral
\begin{align}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \frac{e^{z x}}{x^2} dx.
\end{align}
I honestly don't know how to tackle this, but Mathematica gives the answer as
\begin{align}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \frac{e^{z x}}{x^2} dx = (z + i\omega )\left(\ln (z + i\omega ) - \ln (-(z + i \omega) ) \right).
\end{align}
Let $z = r e^{i \theta}$, where $-\pi <= \theta < \pi$ is the argument of $z$, then the complex log of $z$ is $\ln r + i \theta$. Note that if we negate $z$ then we add $\pi$ to $\theta$. If $-\pi \le \theta < 0$ then we are still located in the principle branch. However if $0 \le \theta < \pi$ then adding $\pi$ will take us out of the principle branch and we need to subtract $2 \pi$. This means 
\begin{align}
    \ln e^{i (\theta  +\pi)} = \begin{cases}
        i (\theta + \pi) & \text{if} \quad -\pi \le \theta < 0 \\
        i (\theta - \pi) & \text{if} \quad 0 \le \theta < \pi.
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
This gives rise to the difference in logarithms as
\begin{align}
    \ln (z) - \ln (-z) = \begin{cases}
        - i \pi & \text{if} \quad -\pi \le \theta < 0 \\
        i \pi & \text{if} \quad 0 \le \theta < \pi.
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
We can simplify this to $\ln(z) - \ln (-z) = i \pi {\rm sign}(\im(z))$. Then our Fourier transform simplifies to
\begin{align}
    \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \frac{e^{z x}}{x^2} dx = \pi (i z - \omega)  {\rm sign}(\im (z) + \omega).
\end{align}
However, we note that this is undefined if $\im(z) + \omega = 0$. 


Plugging this into our expression for $\hat{g}(\omega)$ yields
\begin{align}
    \hat{g}(\omega) &= \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{8}} \left(-\omega {\rm sign}(\omega) - (i \beta - \omega) {\rm sign}(\omega) \right) \\
    &- \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{32}}\left((t - \omega){\rm sign}(-t + \omega) -  (t + \omega){\rm sign}(t + \omega) \right) \\
    &+ \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{32}} \left((i (\beta - i t) - \omega) {\rm sign}(-t + \omega) + (i (\beta + i t) - \omega) {\rm sign}(t + \omega) \right) \\
    &= -i\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{8}} \beta ~{\rm sign}(\omega) + \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{32}} (\abs{t- \omega} + \abs{t + \omega}) + i \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{32}} \beta ({\rm sign}(-t + \omega) + {\rm sign}(t + \omega)) \\
    &- \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{32}} |-t + \omega| - \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{32}} |t + \omega| \\
    &= i \beta \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{8}} \left( -{\rm sign}(\omega) + \frac{1}{2} {\rm sign}(-t + \omega) + \frac{1}{2} {\rm sign} (t + \omega) \right).
\end{align}
We note that if $\abs{\omega} \ge t$ implies ${\rm sign}(\pm t +\omega ) = {\rm sign}(\omega)$, which then leads to $|\omega| \ge t \implies \left( -{\rm sign}(\omega) + \frac{1}{2} {\rm sign}(-t + \omega) + \frac{1}{2} {\rm sign} (t + \omega) \right) = 0$. Likewise, $|\omega| \le t$ implies 
\begin{align}
    \left( -{\rm sign}(\omega) + \frac{1}{2} {\rm sign}(-t + \omega) + \frac{1}{2} {\rm sign} (t + \omega) \right) = (- {\rm sign}(\omega)). 
\end{align}
The final simplification is therefore
\begin{align}
    \hat{g}(\omega) = \begin{cases}
        - i \beta \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{8}} & \text{if} \quad 0 < \omega < t \\
        i \beta \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{8}} & \text{if} \quad -t < \omega < 0 \\
        0 & \text{if} \abs{\omega} > t.
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
There are a couple of edge cases I still need to sort out. 

\subsubsection{Back to Convolution}

First we are interested in computing 
\begin{align}
    (f * g)(\Delta) &= \mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}(f) \cdot \mathcal{F}(g))(\Delta) \\
    &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{i y \Delta} \mathcal{F}(f)(y) \cdot \mathcal{F}(g)(y) dy \\
    &= \frac{i \beta}{2 \pi} \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{8}} \left(\int_{-t}^0 e^{i y \Delta} \mathcal{F}(f)(y) dy - \int_{0}^t e^{i y \Delta} \mathcal{F}(f)(y) dy  \right) \\
    &= \frac{ i \beta}{4 \sqrt{2 \pi}} \left(\int_0^t e^{- i y \Delta} \mathcal{F}(f)(-y) dy - \int_{0}^{t} e^{-i y \Delta} \mathcal{F}(f)(- y) dy \right) \\
    &= \frac{i \beta}{16 \pi} \bigg( \int_{0}^t \int_{- \infty}^{\infty} e^{-i y (\Delta - x)} \Pr[x] (1 + \tanh(\beta x / 2)) dx dy \\
    &- \int_{0}^t \int_{- \infty}^{\infty} e^{i y (\Delta - x)} \Pr[x] (1 + \tanh(\beta x / 2)) dx dy \bigg) \\
    &= \frac{\beta}{8 \pi} \int_{0}^t \int_{- \infty }^{\infty} i\left(e^{-i y (\Delta - x)} - e^{i y(\Delta - x)}\right) \Pr[x] (1 + \tanh(\beta x /2)) dx dy \\
    &= \frac{\beta}{4 \pi} \int_{0}^t \int_{- \infty }^{\infty} \sin( y (\Delta - x)) \Pr[x] (1 + \tanh(\beta x /2)) dx dy. 
\end{align}
One simplification we could make is to perform the $y$ integral
\begin{align}
    (f * g)(\Delta, t) &= \frac{\beta}{4 \pi} \int_{- \infty }^{\infty} \frac{1 - \cos( t (\Delta - x))}{(\Delta - x)} \Pr[x] (1 + \tanh(\beta x /2)) dx \\
    &= \frac{\beta}{4\pi} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{\sin^2 (t (\Delta - x) / 2)}{\Delta - x} \Pr[x] (1 + \tanh(\beta x / 2)) dx \\
    &= \frac{-\beta}{4 \pi} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{\sin^2(t u / 2)}{u} \Pr[\Delta - u] (1 + \tanh(\beta (\Delta - u)/2)) du.
\end{align}


We could use a simple triangle inequality to bound this convolution. This yields
\begin{align}
    \abs{(f * g) (\Delta)} &\le \frac{\beta}{4 \pi} \int_0^t \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \abs{\sin(y(\Delta - x))} \Pr[x] \abs{1 + \tanh(\beta x / 2)} dx dy \\
    &\le \frac{\beta}{2\pi} \int_{0}^t \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \Pr[x] dx dy \\
    &\le \frac{t \beta}{2 \pi}.
\end{align}
This upper bound is nice as it is linear in $t$ and $\beta$ and holds for \emph{arbitrary} distributions. However, as we expect our distributions to either decay or have some sort of compact support we might expect to be able to do better. We find that for a uniform distribution over $2 \norm{H_S}$ we can reduce this bound to logarithmic in $t$ through a cosine integral. Using the distribution
\begin{equation}
    \Pr[x] = \begin{cases}
        \frac{1}{2 \norm{H_S}} & \text{if} \quad x \in [0, 2 \norm{H_S}] \\
        0 & \text{else}
    \end{cases},
\end{equation}
we can compute the upper bound on the convolution as
\begin{align}
    \abs{(f * g)(t, \Delta)} &\le \frac{\beta}{4 \pi} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \abs{\frac{\sin^2(t(\Delta - x)/2)}{\Delta - x}} \abs{\Pr[x]} \abs{1 + \tanh(\beta x / 2)} dx \\
    &\le \frac{\beta}{2\pi} \int_{0}^{2\norm{H_S}} \frac{\sin^2(t(\Delta - x)/2)}{\abs{\Delta - x}} dx \\
    &= \frac{\beta}{2 \pi} \left(\int_0^{\Delta} \frac{\sin^2(t(\Delta - x) / 2)}{\Delta - x} dx + \int_{\Delta}^{2 \norm{H_S}} \frac{\sin^2(t(\Delta - x)/2}{x - \Delta}  dx\right) \\
    &=\frac{\beta}{4 \pi} \left( \int_0^{t \Delta} \frac{1 - \cos(u)}{u} du + \int_{0}^{t (2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)} \frac{1 - \cos (u)}{u} du \right).
\end{align}
This is one of the two different ways to define the ``cosine integral". This is equivalent to
\begin{align}
    \int_{0}^a \frac{1 - \cos(u)}{u} du = \gamma_{\rm E.M.} + \ln a + \int_{a}^{\infty} \frac{\cos(u)}{u}du,
\end{align}
where $\gamma_{\rm E.M.}$ is the Euler-Mascheroni constant. For our purposes we will upper bound it by $3/4$. The other cosine integral is a known quantity, Mathematica claims it reaches a maximum at $a = \frac{\pi}{2}$ and can be upper bounded by 
\begin{align}
    \int_{\pi / 2}^{\infty} \frac{\cos u}{u} du = 0.4720\ldots \le \frac{1}{2}.
\end{align}
This gives our final upper bound as
\begin{align}
    \abs{(f * g)(t, \Delta)} &\le \frac{\beta}{4 \pi} \left( 2 (\gamma_{\rm E.M.} + \frac{1}{2}) + \ln (t \Delta) + \ln (t (2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta))   \right) \\
    &\le \frac{\beta}{4\pi} \left(2 + 2\ln t + \ln \Delta + \ln (2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)  \right).
\end{align}

This is much better, as the $t$ dependence is logarithmic. Even better, the dependence on $\beta$ has been reduced from $e^{\beta \delta_{\min}}$ to just $\beta$. 

The last step is to argue the same result holds for the other transition and to plug it into the summation over $i,j$, using normalization of the density matrix and the completion of the identity trick to get the norm of $G$ out. 

We isolate the on-resonance dynamics
\begin{align}
    \norm{\rho - \Phi(\rho)}_1 &\le \norm{\rho - \rho(t) - \mathcal{T}_{\rm on}(\rho)}_1 + \norm{\mathcal{T}_{\off}(\rho)}_1 + \norm{R_{\Phi}^{(2)}(\rho)}_1 \\
    &\le \norm{\mathcal{T}_{\on}(\rho)}_1 + \frac{8 \alpha^2 \EE_G \norm{G}^2}{ \delta_{\min}^2} + \frac{32}{3} \alpha^3 t^3 \EE_G \norm{G}^3.
\end{align}
These latter two terms can always be made arbitrarily small by sending $\alpha \to 0$. This means our main task is to find the states that have vanishing on-resonance dynamics.



As we have seen the on-resonance dynamics are highly dependent on the value of $\gamma$ that is chosen. As a consequence we will introduce $\gamma$ averaging, where $\gamma$ is randomly chosen from a distribution and the resulting dynamics are averaged. We will let $\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}$ denote the dynamics for a specific value of $\gamma$ and $\EE_\gamma$ the average over all choices. This gives our one norm as
\begin{align}
    \norm{\EE_\gamma \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\rho)}_1 &= \norm{\EE_\gamma \sum_{i, j} \rho_{i,i} \ketbra{j}{j} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{i}{i})\ket{j} }_1 \nonumber \\
    &=\sum_{j} \abs{\sum_{i} \rho_{i,i} \EE_\gamma \bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j}} \\
    &= \sum_j \abs{\EE_{\gamma} \sum_{i \neq j} \rho_{i, i} \bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} + \rho_{j, j} \bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{j} }\\
    &=  \sum_{j} \abs{\EE_{\gamma} \sum_{i \neq j} (\rho_{i,i} \bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} - \rho_{j, j} \bra{i}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{i}}.
\end{align}
We see that each term in the parenthesis is essentially a statement of detailed balance. The total one-norm of the state is then a sum of the deviations of the distribution from satisfying detailed balance. 

This leads to our first fixed point result, that regardless of the environment gap $\gamma$ and the interaction $G$ the ground state is always a fixed point. 
\begin{align}
    &\lim_{\beta \to \infty} \norm{\EE_\gamma \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)} (\ketbra{0}{0})}_1 \nonumber \\
    &\le 2 \lim_{\beta \to \infty} \sum_{j > 0} \bra{j} \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{0}{0})\ket{j} \nonumber \\
    &= 2 \alpha^2 t^2 \sum_{j > 0} \left(\lim_{\beta \to \infty} \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right) \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_{S}(0,j) - \gamma} \le \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(0,j) - \gamma)t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{0,0} G \ket{j,1}}^2 \nonumber \\
    &+ 2 \alpha^2 t^2 \sum_{j > 0} \left(\lim_{\beta \to \infty} \frac{e^{-\beta \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right) \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_{S}(0,j) + \gamma} \le \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(0,j) + \gamma)t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{0,1} G \ket{j,0}}^2 \nonumber \\
    &= 2 \alpha^2 t^2 \sum_{j > 0} \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_{S}(0,j) - \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(0,j) - \gamma)t}{2}\right) \EE_G \abs{\bra{0,0} G \ket{j,1}}^2.
\end{align}
However, we know that $j > 0$ implies that $\Delta_S(0, j) = \lambda_S(0) - \lambda_S(j) \le 0$. Therefore the indicator function can be expressed $\mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(0,j) - \gamma} \ge \delta_{\min} ] = \mathbf{I}[|\Delta_S(0,j)| + \gamma \le \delta_{\min} ]$. However, we have chosen $\delta_{\min} \le |\Delta_S(i,j)|$ for all $(i, j)$ and therefore this condition can never be satisfied. Since the indicator variable is 0 for all $0 \le \gamma$ and $j$ we conclude $\lim_{\beta \to \infty} \norm{\EE_\gamma \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{0}{0})}_1 = 0$ and therefore the ground state is always a fixed point of the on-resonance dynamics.

The ground state being fixed regardless of our knowledge of the Bohr frequencies $\Delta_S(i,j)$ is a rather special property. For finite $\beta$ we can compute the detailed balance deviation as follows. First we simplify the $i > j$ terms as we can drop one of the on-resonance terms from Eq. \eqref{eq:on_resonance} due to $\Delta_S(i,j) > 0$ in this scenario

Likewise for the $i < j$ terms we have
\begin{align}
    &\rho_{i,i} \bra{j}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)} (\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} - \rho_{j,j} \bra{i}\mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\ketbra{j}{j}) \ket{i} \nonumber \\
    &= \alpha^2 t^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \lambda_S (j)}}{\partfun_S} \EE_G \abs{\bra{j,0} G \ket{i, 1}}^2 \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(j,i) - \gamma} \le \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(j,i) - \gamma) t}{2}\right) \left(\frac{e^{-\beta (\gamma - \Delta_S(j,i)} - 1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right)
\end{align}
Now we bring everything back in and introduce the $\gamma$ averaging
\begin{align}
    &\norm{\EE_\gamma \mathcal{T}_{\on}^{(\gamma)}(\rho(\beta))}_1 \\
    &=\alpha^2 t^2 \sum_{j} \bigg|\sum_{i <j} \EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 1}G\ket{j,0}}^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \lambda_S(j)}}{\partfun_S} \EE_\gamma  \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(j,i) - \gamma} \le \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(j,i) - \gamma) t}{2}\right) \left(\frac{e^{\beta ( \Delta_S(j,i) - \gamma} - 1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right) \\
    &- \sum_{i > j} \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,0} G \ket{j,1}}^2 \frac{e^{-\beta \lambda_S(i)}}{\partfun_S} \EE_\gamma \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma} \le \delta_{\min}] \sinc^2\left(\frac{(\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma) t}{2} \right) \frac{ e^{\beta (\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma)} - 1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}}\bigg|.
\end{align}
This means we should best approximate the following expectation
\begin{align}
    &\EE_\gamma \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta - \gamma} \le \delta_{\min}] \frac{e^{\beta (\Delta - \gamma)} - 1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sinc^2 \left(\frac{(\Delta - \gamma)t}{2}\right) \\ 
    &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \Pr[\gamma] \mathbf{I}[\abs{\Delta - \gamma} \le \delta_{\min}] \frac{e^{\beta (\Delta - \gamma)} - 1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sinc^2 \left(\frac{(\Delta - \gamma)t}{2}\right) d \gamma \\
    &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \Pr[\Delta - u] \mathbf{I}[\abs{u} \le \delta_{\min}] \frac{ e^{\beta u} - 1}{1 + e^{\beta (u - \Delta)}} \sinc^2(u t / 2) du \\
    &= \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \Pr[\Delta - u] \frac{ e^{\beta u}  - 1}{1 + e^{\beta (u - \Delta)}} \sinc^2(u t / 2) du.
\end{align}
One thing to note right away is that as $t \to \infty$ we get that the sinc function approaches 0 for all values except $u = 0$. In this scenario we see that the numerator of the fraction in the middle is 0 for u = 0, indicating that the thermal state becomes exactly fixed. Our goal is to determine how small this deviation is, which in turn dictates how close to being fixed the thermal state is. 

Now let's determine just how this integral actually scales. As we are interested in $t$, we see that we can isolate the integral into a function of $t$ and a function solely of $u$ to get
\begin{align}
    \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \Pr[\Delta - u] \frac{ e^{\beta u}  - 1}{1 + e^{\beta (u - \Delta)}} \sinc^2(u t / 2) du &= \frac{4}{t^2} \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \frac{h(u)}{u^2} \sin^2 (u t /2) d u \\
    h(u) &\coloneqq \Pr[\Delta - u] \frac{e^{\beta u } - 1}{1 + e^{\beta(u - \Delta)}} ,
\end{align}
where the $u^2$ in the denominator comes from the $\sinc^2$. factor. Our first note is that $h(0) = 0$, as we pointed out previously. We will take a Taylor Series approximation of $h$ about $u = 0$ and stop at second order. This is expressed
\begin{align}
    h(u) &= h(0) + h'(0) + \int_0^u h''(u') (u - u') du' \nonumber \\
    &= u h'(0) + u^2 \int_0^1 h''(u \cdot x) (1 - x) dx.
\end{align}
As a reminder $h(0) = 0$, we just left it in the expression for clarity.
Plugging in to the integral we get
\begin{align}
    \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \frac{h'(0)}{u} \sin^2(ut /2) du + \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \int_{0}^1 h''(u \cdot x) dx \sin^2(u t /2) du.
\end{align}
We see that the first term is identically 0 as $h'(0)$ does not depend on $u$ and the integrand is odd with respect to $u$ over a symmetric interval. This leaves only the derivative of the curvature to contribute. 

First let's look at what the second derivative actually is
\begin{align}
     \frac{d^2}{du^2} h(u) &=  2\parens{\frac{d}{du} \Pr[\Delta - u] \frac{d}{du} \frac{e^{\beta u} - 1}{1 + e^{\beta (u- \Delta)}}} + \frac{e^{\beta u} - 1}{1 + e^{\beta(u - \Delta)}} \frac{d^2}{du^2} \Pr[\Delta - u] + \Pr[\Delta - u] \frac{d^2}{du^2} \frac{e^{\beta u} - 1}{1 + e^{\beta (u- \Delta)}}
\end{align}

\subsubsection{Uniform distribution}
I can actually make use of the uniform distribution scenario in the mixed gaussian scenario to bound the ``off-resonance" probabilities. This is $\Pr[\Delta - u] = \frac{1}{2 \norm{H_S}}$, which has derivative of 0 (except at the boundary points, we can think of the derivative approaching the boundaries from the interior). Then we have 
\begin{align}
    \frac{d^2}{du^2} h(u) &= \frac{1}{2 \norm{H_S}} \frac{d^2}{du^2} \frac{e^{\beta u} - 1}{1 + e^{\beta (u- \Delta)}} \nonumber \\
    &= \frac{1}{2 \norm{H_S}} 
\end{align}

First a simplification
\begin{align}
    \frac{e^{\beta u} - 1}{1 + e^{\beta (u- \Delta)}} &=  \frac{e^{\beta u/2} (e^{\beta u /2} - e^{-\beta u / 2})}{e^{\beta u /2} e^{-\beta \Delta / 2} (e^{-\beta (u - \Delta) / 2} + e^{\beta (u - \Delta)/2})} = e^{\beta \Delta / 2} \frac{\sinh{\beta u /2}}{\cosh \beta (u-\Delta)/2}.
\end{align}
This gives the second derivative much easier
\begin{align}
    \frac{d^2}{du^2} h(u) &= \frac{\beta}{4 \norm{H_S}} e^{\beta \Delta /2} \frac{d}{du} \left( \frac{\cosh(\beta u /2)}{\cosh(\beta (u - \Delta) / 2)} - \frac{\sinh(\beta u / 2) \sinh(\beta (u - \Delta) / 2}{\cosh(\beta (u - \Delta)/2)^2} \right) \nonumber \\
    &= \frac{\beta^2}{ \norm{H_S}} \left(1 + e^{\beta \Delta}\right)  \frac{\sinh(\beta(u - \Delta)/2)^4}{\sinh(\beta ( u -\Delta))^3}.
\end{align}
Plugging back into the double integral we need to compute
\begin{align}
    \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \int_{0}^1 h''(u \cdot x) dx \sin^2(u t /2) du &= \frac{\beta^2}{\norm{H_S}} \left( 1 + e^{\beta \Delta}\right) \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \int_0^1 \frac{\sinh(\beta(u x - \Delta)/2)^4}{\sinh(\beta ( u x -\Delta))^3} dx \sin^2(u t /2) du \\
    &= \frac{\beta}{4 \norm{H_S}} \frac{1 + e^{\beta \Delta}}{\cosh(\beta \Delta)} \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \frac{1}{u} \left(1 - \frac{\cosh(\beta \Delta)}{\cosh(\beta(\Delta - u)}\right) \sin^2 (u t /2) du,
\end{align}
where the inner integral over $x$ was evaluated using Mathematica. 

The value of this integral will clearly depend on the value of the probability distribution over $\gamma$. I want to analyze this in two regimes, the completely uniform distribution and the mixed Gaussian distribution. We will let
\begin{equation}
    \Pr[\gamma] = \frac{1}{2 \norm{H_S}}
\end{equation}
We can then simplify the integral for this distribution
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{2\norm{H_S}} \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \frac{e^{\beta u} - 1}{1 + e^{\beta(u - \Delta)}} \frac{4 \sin^2(u t /2)}{u^2 t^2} du &= \frac{2}{ \norm{H_S} t^2 } \int_{-\delta_{\min}}^{\delta_{\min}} \frac{e^{\beta u} - 1}{ 1 + e^{\beta (u - \Delta)}} \frac{\sin^2(u t /2)}{u^2} du.
\end{align}



%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\section{Transition Rates}
In this section we investigate the transition rates over random choices of the environment gap $\gamma$. We will study the transition from $\ketbra{i}{i}$ to $\ketbra{j}{j}$ for $\lambda_S(i) > \lambda_S(j)$ and define the energy difference $\Delta = \Delta_S(i,j) = \lambda_S(i) - \lambda_S (j)$. We further introduce the assumption that $G = G_S \tp X$, which allows us to ignore same-energy transitions. These tend to contribute nothing but noise to the dynamics we are actually interested in. 

With this simplification the total transition rate is given by
\begin{align}
    &\EE_\gamma \bra{j} \Phi^{(2)}_\gamma(\ketbra{i}{i}) \ket{j} \nonumber \\
    &= \alpha^2 t^2 \left(\EE_G \abs{\bra{i, 0} G \ket{j, 1}}^2 \EE_\gamma \frac{\sinc^2 ((\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma) t/ 2)}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} + \EE_G \abs{\bra{i,1} G \ket{j, 0}}^2 \EE_\gamma \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta_S(i,j) + \gamma) t/ 2)}{1 + e^{\beta \gamma}}\right). 
\end{align}
We will leave the interaction dependent part for now and instead focus on the behavior with respect to $\gamma$. 

\subsection{Discrete Samples}
Suppose that we have perfect knowledge of a handful of values of $\Delta$ for various $i, j$, letting $\Gamma$ denote this set. This gives rise to the distribution
\begin{align}
    \Pr[\gamma] = \frac{1}{\abs{\Gamma}}\sum_{\gamma' \in \Gamma} \delta(\gamma - \gamma').
\end{align}
We will assume that each of the $\gamma' \in \Gamma$ are positive without loss of generality.

\subsubsection{$i > j$ rates}
This leads to the rather straightforward transition rate of
\begin{align}
    \EE_\gamma \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t/ 2)}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} = \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\sum_{\gamma' \in \gamma} \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t/ 2)}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}}.
\end{align}
We will introduce upper and lower bounds for this rate based determining which values of $\gamma$ are ``sufficiently close" to $\Delta$. Let $\delta$ denote a user-defined parameter such that $\abs{\Delta - \gamma} \le \delta$ implies $\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma)t /2) \ge 1 - \frac{\delta^2 t^2}{2}$. This is proven in Lemma \ref{lem:sinc_poly_approx}. We also have the envelope that $\sinc^2 ((\Delta - \gamma)t /2) \le \frac{4}{(\Delta - \gamma)^2 t^2}$ and the absolute upper bound $\sinc^2(x) \le 1$. 

These inequalities are enough for us to form the following upper and lower bounds. First, the upper bound
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\sum_{\gamma' \in \gamma} \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t/ 2)}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} &= \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\sum_{\gamma \in \Gamma_\delta(\Delta)} \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma)t/2}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} + \frac{1}{|\Gamma|} \sum_{\gamma \in \Gamma \setminus \Gamma_\delta(\Delta)} \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma)t/2}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \\
    &\le \frac{|\Gamma_\delta(\Delta)|}{|\Gamma|} + \frac{1}{|\Gamma|} \sum_{\gamma \in \Gamma \setminus \Gamma_\delta(\Delta)} \frac{4}{(\Delta - \gamma)^2 t^2} \\
    &\le \frac{\abs{\Gamma_\delta(\Delta)}}{\abs{\Gamma}} + \frac{|\Gamma| - |\Gamma_\delta(\Delta)|}{|\Gamma|} \frac{4}{\delta^2 t^2} \\
    &= \frac{4}{\delta^2 t^2} + \frac{\abs{\Gamma_\delta(\Delta)}}{\abs{\Gamma}} \left( 1 - \frac{4}{\delta^2 t^2} \right).
\end{align}
Next we have the lower bound
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{|\Gamma|} \sum_{\gamma' \in \Gamma} \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma)t/2)}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} &\ge \frac{1}{|\Gamma|} \sum_{\gamma' \in \Gamma_\delta(\Delta)} \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma)t/2)}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \\
    &\ge \frac{|\Gamma_\delta(\Delta)|}{2|\Gamma|} \left(1 - \frac{\delta^2 t^2}{2}\right).
\end{align}
In a previous iteration of this paper we chose $\Gamma$ to be the set of positive Bohr frequencies of the Hamiltonian and we let $\delta$ be small enough such that $\Gamma_\delta(\Delta)$ would always consist of just a single Bohr frequency. This let us use the transition rate of $\frac{2}{\dim_S(\dim_S - 1)}$. 

The other term in the final transition rate will be negligible for $i > j$
\begin{align}
    \EE_\gamma \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta + \gamma) t/2)}{1 + e^{+\beta \gamma}} &= \frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\sum_{\gamma' \in \Gamma} \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta + \gamma')t/2)}{1 + e^{\beta \gamma'}} \\
    &\le \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta + \gamma_{\min})t/2)}{1+e^{\beta \gamma_{\min}}},
\end{align}
where $\gamma_{\min}$ is the smallest $\gamma' \in \Gamma$. This bound vanishes as $\beta \to \infty$ and $t \to \infty$. 



\subsection{Uniform Approach}
Let $\gamma$ be chosen uniformly from a distribution
\begin{align}
    \Pr[\gamma] = \frac{1}{2\norm{H_S}} \Theta_H(\gamma) \Theta_H(2 \norm{H_S} - \gamma).
\end{align}
The factor of $2$ is necessary as the spectrum of $H_S$ could consist of eigenvalues $\pm \norm{H_S}$ and we would like for all possible pairs to have non-vanishing rates. We will ignore the temperature dependence of the transition rates, as they contribute at most a factor of $2$, as seen in the inequality
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{2} &\le \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \le 1 \\
    \implies \frac{1}{2}\EE_\gamma \sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t/ 2) &\le  \EE_\gamma \frac{\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t/ 2)}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \le \EE_\gamma \sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t/ 2).
\end{align}
We therefore focus on the expected value of the $\sinc^2$ function. For the uniform distribution this can be solved using integration by parts
\begin{align}
&\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \Pr[\gamma]\sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t/ 2) d\gamma \\
&= \frac{1}{2 \norm{H_S}} \int_0^{2 \norm{H_S}} \sinc^2 ((\Delta - \gamma) t /2) d \gamma \\
&= \frac{1}{t \norm{H_S}}  \int_{- (2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta) t /2}^{\Delta t /2} \sinc^2 (u) d u \\
&= \frac{1}{t \norm{H_S}} \left( I_{\sin}\left((2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)t \right) + I_{\rm sin}(\Delta t) - \frac{1- \cos((2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)t}{(2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)t} - \frac{1- \cos(\Delta t))}{\Delta t} \right), 
\end{align}
where $I_{\rm sin}$ is the standard sine integral
\begin{align}
    I_{\sin}(x) = \int_{0}^{x} \frac{\sin u}{u} du.
\end{align}
Note that in the limit as $x \to \infty$ we have $\lim_{x \to \infty} I_{\sin}(x) = \frac{\pi}{2}$. This indicates that the sine integral is the leading term asymptotically in terms of $t$. Further, since $0 \le 1 - \cos(x) \le 2$ we can therefore just drop the negative terms as the denominators are always positive as well, leading to 
\begin{align}
    \EE_\gamma \sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t/ 2) \le \frac{1}{t \norm{H_S}} \left( I_{\sin}\left((2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)t \right) + I_{\rm sin}(\Delta t) \right).
\end{align}
If we wanted to be really precise, we could argue that $t > \frac{1}{\epsilon \min(\Delta, (2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)) }$ and $\epsilon \le \pi / 8$ or something is probably sufficient to argue that $\abs{\EE_\gamma \sinc^2((\Delta - \gamma) t /2) - \frac{\pi}{t \norm{H_S}}} \le 2 \epsilon$. 

\subsubsection{Off-resonance transitions} 
We also need to bound the transitions that violate energy. For this we can use the bound
\begin{align}
    \EE_\gamma \frac{1}{2} \sinc^2((\Delta + \gamma) t/ 2) (1- \tanh{\beta \gamma /2)} & \le \frac{\sinc^2(\Delta t /2)}{2} \EE_\gamma (1 - \tanh(\beta \gamma /2 )) \\
    &=\frac{\sinc^2(\Delta t /2)}{2 \norm{H_S}} (\norm{H_S} - \frac{1}{\beta} \log( \cosh(\beta \norm{H_S})) )\\
    &=\frac{\sinc^2(\Delta t / 2)}{2 \beta \norm{H_S}} \log \left( \frac{2}{1 + e^{-\beta 2 \norm{H_S}}}\right).
\end{align}
We assumed that $\Delta >0$ along with $\gamma > 0$. For $\Delta < 0$ we would need to recompute these bounds. 

\subsection{Convolution Based Approach}
Focusing on the first term that appears in the transition rate reveals a convolution
\begin{align}
    \EE_\gamma \frac{\sinc^2 ((\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma) t/ 2)}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} &= \int_{- \infty}^{\infty} \frac{\Pr[\gamma]}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \sinc^2 ((\Delta_S(i,j) - \gamma) t/ 2) d\gamma \\
    &= \left(\frac{\Pr[x]}{1 + e^{-\beta x}} * \sinc^2\left(\frac{t x }{2} \right) \right) (\Delta).
\end{align}
If we let $\mathcal{F}$ denote the Fourier transform then the convolution theorem tells us $f* g = \mathcal{F}^{-1} (\mathcal{F}(f) \cdot \mathcal{F}(g))$, so we can write the transition amplitude as
\begin{align}
    \left(\frac{\Pr[x]}{1 + e^{-\beta x}} * \sinc^2\left(\frac{t x }{2} \right) \right) (\Delta) &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{- i \Delta x} \mathcal{F}\left(\frac{\Pr[\gamma]}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} \right)(x) \cdot \mathcal{F} \left(\sinc^2 \left(\frac{t \gamma }{2} \right)\right)(x) dx.
\end{align}
Our problem can therefore be reduced to three Fourier transformations. 

First we investigate the sinc function as this has a nice closed form expression. 
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{F}(\sinc^2(t x / 2) )(\omega) &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi}} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega x} \sinc^2(tx /2 ) dx \\
    &= \frac{1}{t^2} \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}} \left(- 2 \abs{\omega} + \abs{\omega - t} + \abs{\omega + t}\right)\\
    &= \frac{\sqrt{2 \pi}}{t} \max\left(0,1 - \frac{\abs{\omega}}{t} \right).
\end{align}
The second line is computed via Mathematica and the third line can be derived on a case by case basis. Note that this function is 0 for $\abs{\omega} \ge t$, so it acts as a window in Fourier space. 

The remaining Fourier transformation of the probability distribution is much harder. To give an example, the Gaussian distribution $\Pr[\gamma] = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi \sigma^2}} e^{-(\gamma - \mu)^2 / 2 \sigma^2}$ gives rise to the following integrals
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{F}\left(\frac{\Pr[\gamma]}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}}\right) &= \frac{1}{2 \pi \sigma} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-i \omega \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} e^{-(\gamma - \mu)^2 / 2 \sigma^2} d \gamma \\
    &= \frac{1}{2 \pi \sigma}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^2} \gamma^2 + \left(\frac{\mu}{\sigma^2} - i \omega \right) \gamma - \frac{\mu^2}{2 \sigma^2}} }{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} d \gamma \\
    &= \frac{e^{-\frac{\mu^2}{2 \sigma^2}}}{2 \pi \sigma} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{e^{- \frac{1}{2 \sigma^2} \gamma^2 + \left(\frac{\mu}{\sigma^2} -i \omega + \frac{\beta}{2}\right) \gamma}}{\cosh(\beta \gamma /2)} d\gamma \\
    &= \frac{e^{-\mu^2 / 2 \sigma^2}}{\sigma \beta} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{e^{- \frac{\pi^2}{\sigma^2 \beta^2} x^2 + 2 \pi \left( \frac{\mu}{\sigma^2 \beta} - i \frac{\omega}{\beta} + \frac{1}{2} \right) x}}{\cosh(\pi x)} dx.
\end{align}
This last integral is equal to the Mordell integral $h(\tau; z)$, where $\tau = \frac{i\pi}{\sigma^2 \beta^2}$ and $z = -\frac{2\mu + \sigma^2 \beta}{2\sigma^2 \beta} +i \frac{\omega}{\beta}$, see Zweger \cite{zwegers2008mock} for its connections to Mock Theta functions. This integral has no closed form expression but it has been studied in a diverse range of situations, from black hole physics \cite{dabholkar2012quantum}, supersymmetry \cite{russo2015supersymmetric}, and electrode catalysis \cite{zeng2014electrocatalysis}. 

In the effort to derive closed form solutions, we will instead use the simpler uniform distribution. Due to a lack of any information on the Bohr frequency we will use $\Pr[\gamma] = \frac{1}{2 \norm{H_S}} \Theta_H(\gamma) \Theta_H(2 \norm{H_S} - \gamma)$, where $\Theta_H$ is the Heaviside theta function. Any upper bound on the spectral norm of $H_S$ will suffice, we double it as it is possible for the spectrum of $H_S$ to consist of an eigenvalue at $- \norm{H_S}$ and one at $\norm{H_S}$ and we would like for this to also be thermalized. 

This distribution leads to the Fourier transform 
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{F}\left(\frac{\Pr[\gamma]}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}}\right) &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-i \omega \gamma} \frac{\Theta_H(\gamma) \Theta_H(2 \norm{H_S} - \gamma)}{2 \norm{H_S}} \frac{e^{-i \omega \gamma}}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} d \gamma \\
    &= \frac{1}{4 \norm{H_S} \sqrt{2 \pi}} \int_0^{2\norm{H_S}} e^{- i \omega \gamma} (1  +\tanh(\beta x /2) d\gamma,
\end{align}
where we have made the following substitution
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\beta \gamma}} &= e^{\beta \gamma /2 } \frac{1}{e^{\beta \gamma / 2} + e^{-\beta \gamma /2}} = \frac{\cosh(\beta \gamma /2) + \sinh(\beta \gamma /2)}{2 \cosh(\beta \gamma /2)} = \frac{1}{2} (1  +\tanh(\beta \gamma /2)).
\end{align}
This integral can be expressed in terms of special functions as
\begin{align}
    &\int_{0}^{2 \norm{H_S}} e^{- i \omega \gamma} (1  +\tanh(\beta x /2) d\gamma \\
    &= 2i \frac{e^{-i \omega 2 \norm{H_S}} - 1}{\omega} + \frac{1}{\beta} \left( \psi_G\parens{\frac{1}{2} - i \frac{\omega}{2 \beta}} - \psi_G\parens{- i \frac{\omega}{2 \beta}} - 2 e^{- \pi \omega / \beta} {\rm Beta}\left(e^{- \beta 2 \norm{H_S}} , -i \frac{\omega}{\beta}\right)\right),
\end{align}
where $\psi_G$ is the digamma function and Beta is Euler's Beta function. For now, we will collectively denote these terms as
\begin{equation}
    f(\beta, \omega) \coloneqq \psi_G\parens{\frac{1}{2} - i \frac{\omega}{2 \beta}} - \psi_G\parens{- i \frac{\omega}{2 \beta}} - 2 e^{- \pi \omega / \beta} {\rm Beta} \left(e^{- \beta 2 \norm{H_S}} , -i \frac{\omega}{\beta}\right)
\end{equation}
for simplicity. 

Now we have enough information to plug in for the last Fourier transform to compute the convolution, which is given by
\begin{align}
    &\mathcal{F}^{-1} \left(\mathcal{F}(\Pr[\gamma]/(1 + e^{-\beta \gamma} ) \cdot \mathcal{F}(\sinc^2(x t /2) \right) \\
    &= \frac{1}{4 \sqrt{2 \pi} \norm{H_S} } \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{i \Delta \omega} \left(2 i \frac{e^{-i \omega 2 \norm{H_S}} - 1}{\omega} + \frac{f(\beta, \omega)}{\beta} \right) \frac{\max\left(0, 1 - \frac{|\omega|}{t} \right)}{t} d\omega \\
    &= \frac{1}{4 \sqrt{2 \pi} \norm{H_S}} \frac{2i}{t} \left(\int_{-t}^t \frac{e^{-i (2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)\omega} - 1}{\omega} \left(1 - \frac{|\omega|}{t}\right) d\omega \right) + \frac{1}{\beta t} \int_{-t}^t e^{i \Delta \omega} f(\beta, \omega) \left(1 - \frac{|\omega|}{t}\right) d\omega.
\end{align}
We see that this splits into two parts, a temperature independent term and a temperature dependent term. Focusing first on the temperature independent part we get
\begin{align}
    &\frac{1}{4 \sqrt{2 \pi} \norm{H_S}} \frac{2i}{t} \int_{-t}^{t} \frac{e^{-i (2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta) \omega} - 1}{\omega} \left(1 - \frac{|\omega|}{t}\right) d\omega \\
    &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \frac{1}{2 \norm{H_S}} \left(-(2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta) \sinc^2((2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta) t/2) + \frac{2}{t} I_{\rm sin}((2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta) t) \right),
\end{align}

The large $t$ behavior of this integral is $\lim_{t \to \infty} I_{\sin}((2 \norm{H_S} - \Delta)t) = \frac{\pi}{2}$. This means that as $t \to \infty$ the temperature independent component scales as $O(1/t)$. 

The temperature dependent component is a different beast. 

\section{Convergence Rates}
Now that we have a notion of approximate fixed points and the magnitude of the transition rates, we can tie it all together to get convergence bounds. As we know that to second order we have a Markov matrix we should be able to bound the convergence rate to a fixed point using spectral information of the transition matrix. However, one major disadvantage to our dynamics is that they are, by design, non-reversible. To see this, as we send $\beta \to \infty$ and each input state relaxes to the lowest energy states in it's support as we increase the number of interactions. For an ergodic chain this means that each input state converges to the ground state manifold and we would be unable to reverse this process as we would have no information about the input state. This non-reversibility is a bit of an issue as the vast majority of generic Markov chain results are stated for reversible systems.

In a previous iteration of this paper we used a generic bound due to Jerison \cite{jerison2013general} that works for arbitrary non-reversible Markov chains. This bound had the advantage that it relied on eigenvalues that were readily computable in the ground state limit. The downside of this bound is that it depended quasi-linearly on the inverse of the spectral gap and had an explicit dependence on the dimension of the Hilbert space. In this iteration we seek to use newer bounds for non-reversible Markov chains due to Chatterjee \cite{chatterjee2025spectral} that do not explicitly depend on the dimension but instead have a number of tradeoffs. The first is that they depend not on the eigenvalue spectral gap of the transition matrix for the Markov chain but instead on the singular value gap of the generator $I - M$. The second is that they introduce explicit bounds that depend on the log of the minimum probability of the resulting fixed point, which for a thermal distribution looks like $\log \partfun_S + \beta \norm{H_S}$. Our goal for the remainder of this section is to tie these pieces together to get concrete bounds on the number of interactions needed, the coupling strength $\alpha$, and the time per interaction needed to reach thermalization.

To start we reduce the convergence of the quantum channel to the Markov chain
\begin{align}
    \norm{\rho - \Phi^{\circ N}(\rho)}_1 &=  \norm{\rho - \Phi^{\circ N}(\rho) + \left(\id + \mathcal{T}\right)^{\circ N}(\rho) - \left(\id + \mathcal{T}\right)^{\circ N}(\rho)}_1 \\
    &\le \norm{\rho - \left(\id + \mathcal{T}\right)^{\circ N} \rho}_1 + N \norm{R_{\Phi}(\rho)}_1 \\
    &= \norm{\vec{p} - (I + T)^N \vec{p}}_1 + N \norm{R_\Phi (\rho)}_1.
\end{align}
As we already have the remainder norm bounded we then just need to bound the convergence to the fixed state of the Markov chain $M =I + T$. A standard approach would bound the eigenvalue gap of $M$ and use standard mixing time results. However, these approaches typically assume reversibility of the Markov chain, which we do not have. In a previous iteration of this paper we used a bound that worked for arbitrary Markov chains at the downside of introducing explicit dimensionful factors. In this iteration we seek to improve these results using new bounds due to Chatterjee \cite{chatterjee2025spectral} that are based on singular values of generators derived from $M$ and avoid these dimensionful factors.
